					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content"><div class="mw-subjectpageheader">
</div><div class="mw-content-ltr mw-parser-output" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Person who helps others learn</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1320445320">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}@media print{body.ns-0 .mw-parser-output .hatnote{display:none!important}}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Educators" redirects here. For the TV series, see <a href="/wiki/Educators_(TV_series)" class="mw-redirect" title="Educators (TV series)"><i>Educators</i> (TV series)</a>.</div> 
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Teacher_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Teacher (disambiguation)">Teacher (disambiguation)</a>, <a href="/wiki/Teachers_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Teachers (disambiguation)">Teachers (disambiguation)</a>, <a href="/wiki/Headmaster_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Headmaster (disambiguation)">Headmaster (disambiguation)</a>, <a href="/wiki/Professor" title="Professor">Professor</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tutor" class="mw-redirect" title="Tutor">Tutor</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Paraprofessional_educator" title="Paraprofessional educator">Paraprofessional educator</a>.</div> 
<p class="mw-empty-elt">

</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1316064257">.mw-parser-output .infobox-subbox{padding:0;border:none;margin:-3px;width:auto;min-width:100%;font-size:100%;clear:none;float:none;background-color:transparent;color:inherit}.mw-parser-output <figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Brack_Vocabularius_rerum.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Brack_Vocabularius_rerum.jpg/250px-Brack_Vocabularius_rerum.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="264" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Brack_Vocabularius_rerum.jpg/330px-Brack_Vocabularius_rerum.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Brack_Vocabularius_rerum.jpg/500px-Brack_Vocabularius_rerum.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1112" data-file-height="1728" /></a><figcaption>A teacher of a Latin school and two students, 1487</figcaption></figure>
<p>A <b>teacher</b>, also called a <b>schoolteacher</b> or formally an <b>educator</b>, is a person who helps <a href="/wiki/Student" title="Student">students</a> to acquire <a href="/wiki/Knowledge" title="Knowledge">knowledge</a>, competence, or <a href="/wiki/Virtue" title="Virtue">virtue</a>, via the practice of <a href="/wiki/Teaching" title="Teaching">teaching</a>.
</p><p><i>Informally</i> the role of teacher may be taken on by anyone (e.g. when showing a colleague how to perform a specific task). 
In some countries, teaching young people of school age may be carried out in an informal setting, such as within the family (<a href="/wiki/Homeschooling" title="Homeschooling">homeschooling</a>), rather than in a formal setting such as a school or college. 
Some other professions may involve a significant amount of teaching (e.g. youth worker, pastor).
</p><p>In most countries, <i>formal</i> teaching of students is usually carried out by paid professional teachers. This article focuses on those who are <i>employed</i>, as their main role, to teach others in a <i>formal</i> <a href="/wiki/Education" title="Education">education</a> context, such as at a school or other place of <i>initial</i> formal education or training.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Duties_and_functions">Duties and functions</h2></div>
<p>A teacher's role may vary among cultures.
</p><p>Teachers may provide instruction in <a href="/wiki/Literacy_education" class="mw-redirect" title="Literacy education">literacy</a> and <a href="/wiki/Numeracy" title="Numeracy">numeracy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Artisan" title="Artisan">craftsmanship</a> or <a href="/wiki/Vocational_training" class="mw-redirect" title="Vocational training">vocational training</a>, <a href="/wiki/The_arts" title="The arts">the arts</a>, <a href="/wiki/Religion" title="Religion">religion</a>, <a href="/wiki/Civics" title="Civics">civics</a>, community roles, or life skills.
</p><p>Formal teaching tasks include preparing lessons according to agreed curricula, giving lessons, and assessing pupil progress.
</p><p>A teacher's professional duties may extend beyond formal teaching. Outside of the classroom teachers may accompany students on field trips, supervise <a href="/wiki/Study_hall" title="Study hall">study halls</a>, help with the organization of school functions, and serve as supervisors for <a href="/wiki/Extracurricular_activities" class="mw-redirect" title="Extracurricular activities">extracurricular activities</a>. They also have the legal duty to protect students from harm,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>2<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> such as that which may result from bullying,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>3<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> sexual harassment, racism or abuse.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>4<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
In some education systems, teachers may be responsible for student <a href="/wiki/Discipline" title="Discipline">discipline</a>.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Competences_and_qualities_required_by_teachers">Competences and qualities required by teachers</h2></div>
<p>Teaching is a highly complex activity.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Caena&#95;5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Caena-5"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>5<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
This is partially because teaching is a social practice, that takes place in a specific context (time, place, culture, socioeconomic situation etc.) and therefore is shaped by the values of that specific context.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Cochrane&#95;6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cochrane-6"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>6<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Factors that influence what is expected (or required) of teachers include history and tradition, social views about the purpose of education, accepted theories about learning, etc.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Cummings&#95;7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cummings-7"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>7<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Competences">Competences</h3></div>
<p>The competences required by a teacher are affected by the different ways in which the role is understood around the world. Broadly, there seem to be four models: 
</p>
<dl><dd>the teacher as manager of instruction;</dd>
<dd>the teacher as caring person;</dd>
<dd>the teacher as expert learner; and</dd>
<dd>the teacher as cultural and civic person.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Caena2&#95;8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Caena2-8"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>8<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></dd></dl>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Organisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development" class="mw-redirect" title="Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development">Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development</a> has argued that it is necessary to develop a shared definition of the skills and knowledge required by teachers, in order to guide teachers' career-long education and professional development.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-OECD&#95;9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-OECD-9"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>9<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Some evidence-based international discussions have tried to reach such a common understanding. For example, the <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a> has identified three broad areas of competences that teachers require:
</p>
<dl><dd>Working with others</dd>
<dd>Working with knowledge, technology, and information, and</dd>
<dd>Working in and with society.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Caena3&#95;10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Caena3-10"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>10<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></dd></dl>
<p>Scholarly consensus is emerging that what is required of teachers can be grouped under three headings:
</p>
<dl><dd>knowledge (such as: the subject matter itself and knowledge about how to teach it, curricular knowledge, knowledge about the educational sciences, psychology, assessment etc.)</dd>
<dd>craft skills (such as lesson planning, using teaching technologies, managing students and groups, monitoring and assessing learning etc.) and</dd>
<dd>dispositions (such as essential values and attitudes, beliefs and commitment).<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Will&#95;11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Will-11"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>11<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></dd></dl>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Qualities">Qualities</h3></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Enthusiasm">Enthusiasm</h4></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ashs-teacher-and-students.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Ashs-teacher-and-students.jpg/250px-Ashs-teacher-and-students.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="160" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Ashs-teacher-and-students.jpg/500px-Ashs-teacher-and-students.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="410" /></a><figcaption>A teacher interacts with older students at a school in New Zealand.</figcaption></figure>
<p>It has been found that teachers who showed enthusiasm towards the course materials and students can create a positive learning experience.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>12<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> These teachers do not teach by rote but attempt to invigorate their teaching of the course materials every day.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>13<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Teachers who cover the same curriculum repeatedly may find it challenging to maintain their enthusiasm, lest their boredom with the content bore their students in turn. Enthusiastic teachers are rated higher by their students than teachers who did not show much enthusiasm for the course materials.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>14<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mr._Shake_Amargosa.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Mr._Shake_Amargosa.jpg/250px-Mr._Shake_Amargosa.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="188" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Mr._Shake_Amargosa.jpg/500px-Mr._Shake_Amargosa.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="600" /></a><figcaption>A primary school teacher on a picnic with her students, <a href="/wiki/Colombia" title="Colombia">Colombia</a>, 2014</figcaption></figure>
<p>Teachers that exhibit enthusiasm are more likely to have engaged, interested and energetic students who are curious about learning the subject matter. Recent research has found a correlation between teacher enthusiasm and students' intrinsic motivation to learn and vitality in the classroom.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-auto&#95;15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-15"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>15<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Controlled, experimental studies exploring intrinsic motivation of college students has shown that nonverbal expressions of enthusiasm, such as demonstrative gesturing, dramatic movements which are varied, and emotional facial expressions, result in college students reporting higher levels of intrinsic motivation to learn.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>16<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> But even while a teacher's enthusiasm has been shown to improve motivation and increase task engagement, it does not necessarily improve learning outcomes or memory for the material.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>17<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>There are various mechanisms by which teacher enthusiasm may facilitate higher levels of intrinsic motivation.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>18<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> 
Teacher enthusiasm may contribute to a classroom atmosphere of energy and enthusiasm which feeds student interest and excitement in learning the subject matter.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>19<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Enthusiastic teachers may also lead to students becoming more self-determined in their own learning process. The concept of mere exposure indicates that the teacher's enthusiasm may contribute to the student's expectations about intrinsic motivation in the context of learning. Also, enthusiasm may act as a "motivational embellishment", increasing a student's interest by the variety, novelty, and surprise of the enthusiastic teacher's presentation of the material. Finally, the concept of emotional contagion may also apply: students may become more intrinsically motivated by catching onto the enthusiasm and energy of the teacher.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-auto&#95;15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-15"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>15<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Interaction_with_learners">Interaction with learners</h4></div>
<p>Research shows that student motivation and attitudes towards school are closely linked to student-teacher relationships. Enthusiastic teachers are particularly good at creating beneficial relations with their students. Their ability to create effective learning environments that foster student achievement depends on the kind of relationship they build with their students.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>20<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Bryant,&#95;Jennings&#95;1980&#95;21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bryant,_Jennings_1980-21"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>21<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>22<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Hartmut,&#95;J.&#95;1978&#95;23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hartmut,_J._1978-23"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>23<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Useful teacher-to-student interactions are crucial in linking academic success with personal achievement.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Osborne,&#95;E.&#95;1999&#95;24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Osborne,_E._1999-24"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>24<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Here, personal success is a student's internal goal of improving themselves, whereas academic success includes the goals they receive from their superior. A teacher must guide their student in aligning their personal goals with their academic goals. Students who receive this positive influence show stronger self-confidence and greater personal and academic success than those without these teacher interactions.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Hartmut,&#95;J.&#95;1978&#95;23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hartmut,_J._1978-23"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>23<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-ReferenceA&#95;25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-25"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>25<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>26<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Students are likely to build stronger relations with teachers who are friendly and supportive and will show more interest in courses taught by these teachers.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Osborne,&#95;E.&#95;1999&#95;24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Osborne,_E._1999-24"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>24<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-ReferenceA&#95;25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-25"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>25<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Teachers that spend more time interacting and working directly with students are perceived as supportive and effective teachers. Effective teachers have been shown to invite student participation and decision making, allow humor into their classroom, and demonstrate a willingness to play.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Bryant,&#95;Jennings&#95;1980&#95;21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bryant,_Jennings_1980-21"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>21<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Neutrality">Neutrality</h4></div>
<p>Teachers are generally expected to teach <a href="/wiki/Controversy" title="Controversy">controversial</a> issues with a neutral or impartial pedagogical approach.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>27<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Bias by teachers can result in an <a href="/wiki/Hidden_curriculum" title="Hidden curriculum">hidden curriculum</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>28<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Discrimination by teachers can contribute to <a href="/wiki/Sex_differences_in_education" title="Sex differences in education">sex differences in education</a>. In some countries teachers show a <a href="/wiki/Political_bias" title="Political bias">political bias</a> in political party preference compared to the voters.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-m435&#95;29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-m435-29"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>29<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Teaching_qualifications">Teaching qualifications</h2></div>
<p>In many countries, a person who wishes to become a teacher must first obtain <a href="/wiki/Certified_teacher" title="Certified teacher">specified professional qualifications or credentials</a> from a <a href="/wiki/University" title="University">university</a> or <a href="/wiki/College" title="College">college</a>. These professional qualifications may include the study of <a href="/wiki/Pedagogy" title="Pedagogy">pedagogy</a>, the science of teaching. 
Teachers, like other professionals, may have to, or choose to, continue their education after they qualify, a process known as <a href="/wiki/Continuing_professional_development" class="mw-redirect" title="Continuing professional development">continuing professional development</a>.
</p><p>The issue of teacher qualifications is linked to the status of the profession. In some societies, teachers enjoy a status on a par with <a href="/wiki/Physician" class="mw-redirect" title="Physician">physicians</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lawyer" title="Lawyer">lawyers</a>, <a href="/wiki/Engineer" title="Engineer">engineers</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Accountants" class="mw-redirect" title="Accountants">accountants</a>, in others, the status of the profession is low. In the twentieth century, many intelligent women were unable to get jobs in corporations or governments so many chose teaching as a default profession. As women become more welcomed into corporations and governments today, it may be more difficult to attract qualified teachers in the future.
</p><p>Teachers are often required to undergo a course of initial education at a <a href="/wiki/College_of_Education" class="mw-redirect" title="College of Education">College of Education</a> to ensure that they possess the necessary knowledge, competences and adhere to relevant <a href="/wiki/Ethical_code" title="Ethical code">codes of ethics</a>.
</p><p>There are a variety of bodies designed to instill, preserve and update the knowledge and professional standing of teachers. Around the world many teachers' colleges exist; they may be controlled by government or by the teaching profession itself.
</p><p>They are generally established to serve and protect the public interest through <a href="/wiki/Certify" class="mw-redirect" title="Certify">certifying</a>, governing, quality controlling, and enforcing standards of practice for the teaching profession.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Professional_standards">Professional standards</h3></div>
<p>The functions of the teachers' colleges may include setting out clear standards of practice, providing for the ongoing education of teachers, investigating complaints involving members, conducting hearings into <a href="/wiki/Allegation" title="Allegation">allegations</a> of professional misconduct and taking appropriate disciplinary action and <a href="/wiki/Accredit" class="mw-redirect" title="Accredit">accrediting</a> teacher education programs. In many situations teachers in <a href="/wiki/Public_school_(government_funded)" class="mw-redirect" title="Public school (government funded)">publicly funded schools</a> must be members in good standing with the college, and <a href="/wiki/Private_school" title="Private school">private schools</a> may also require their teachers to be college members. In other areas these roles may belong to the State <a href="/wiki/Board_of_Education" class="mw-redirect" title="Board of Education">Board of Education</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Superintendent_of_Public_Instruction" class="mw-redirect" title="Superintendent of Public Instruction">Superintendent of Public Instruction</a>, the <a href="/wiki/State_Education_Agency" class="mw-redirect" title="State Education Agency">State Education Agency</a> or other governmental bodies. In still other areas <a href="/wiki/Teaching_Union" class="mw-redirect" title="Teaching Union">Teaching Unions</a> may be responsible for some or all of these duties.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Professional_misconduct">Professional misconduct</h2></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Child_abuse" title="Child abuse">Child abuse</a></div>
<p>Misconduct by teachers, especially <a href="/wiki/Sexual_misconduct" title="Sexual misconduct">sexual misconduct</a>, has been getting increased scrutiny from the media and the courts.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>30<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> A study by the <a href="/wiki/American_Association_of_University_Women" title="American Association of University Women">American Association of University Women</a> reported that 9.6% of students in the United States claim to have received unwanted sexual attention from an adult associated with education; be they a volunteer, bus driver, teacher, administrator or other adult; sometime during their educational career.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-DOEd&#95;31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DOEd-31"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>31<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>A study in England showed a 0.3% prevalence of sexual abuse by any professional, a group that included <a href="/wiki/Priest" title="Priest">priests</a>, religious leaders, and case workers as well as teachers.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>32<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> This British study is the only one of its kind and consisted of "a random ... probability sample of 2,869 young people between the ages of 18 and 24 in a computer-assisted study" and that the questions referred to "sexual abuse with a professional," not necessarily a teacher. It is therefore logical to conclude that information on the percentage of abuses by teachers in the United Kingdom is not explicitly available and therefore not necessarily reliable. The AAUW study, however, posed questions about fourteen types of sexual harassment and various degrees of frequency and included only abuses by teachers. "The sample was drawn from a list of 80,000 schools to create a stratified two-stage sample design of 2,065 8th to 11th grade students". Its reliability was gauged at 95% with a 4% margin of error.
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a> especially, several high-profile cases such as <a href="/wiki/Debra_LaFave" class="mw-redirect" title="Debra LaFave">Debra LaFave</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pamela_Rogers_Turner" class="mw-redirect" title="Pamela Rogers Turner">Pamela Rogers Turner</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Mary_Kay_Letourneau" title="Mary Kay Letourneau">Mary Kay Letourneau</a> have caused increased scrutiny on teacher misconduct.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Chris_Keates" title="Chris Keates">Chris Keates</a>, the general secretary of <a href="/wiki/National_Association_of_Schoolmasters_Union_of_Women_Teachers" class="mw-redirect" title="National Association of Schoolmasters Union of Women Teachers">National Association of Schoolmasters Union of Women Teachers</a>, said that teachers who have sex with pupils over the age of consent should not be placed on the sex offenders register and that prosecution for statutory rape "is a real anomaly in the law that we are concerned about." This has led to outrage from child protection and parental rights groups.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>33<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Fears of being labelled a <a href="/wiki/Pedophile" class="mw-redirect" title="Pedophile">pedophile</a> or <a href="/wiki/Hebephile" class="mw-redirect" title="Hebephile">hebephile</a> has led to several men who enjoy teaching avoiding the profession.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>34<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> This has in some jurisdictions reportedly led to a shortage of male teachers.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>35<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Pedagogy_and_teaching">Pedagogy and teaching</h2></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Pedagogy" title="Pedagogy">Pedagogy</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Adriaen_van_Ostade_007.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Adriaen_van_Ostade_007.jpg/250px-Adriaen_van_Ostade_007.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="299" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Adriaen_van_Ostade_007.jpg/500px-Adriaen_van_Ostade_007.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="1576" data-file-height="1883" /></a><figcaption>Dutch schoolmaster and children, 1662</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Teacher_in_Laos.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Teacher_in_Laos.jpg/250px-Teacher_in_Laos.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="188" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Teacher_in_Laos.jpg/500px-Teacher_in_Laos.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="3072" data-file-height="2304" /></a><figcaption>A primary school teacher in northern <a href="/wiki/Laos" title="Laos">Laos</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Rostock_Schmidt_Lehrer-Student.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Rostock_Schmidt_Lehrer-Student.jpg/250px-Rostock_Schmidt_Lehrer-Student.jpg" decoding="async" width="190" height="253" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Rostock_Schmidt_Lehrer-Student.jpg/330px-Rostock_Schmidt_Lehrer-Student.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Rostock_Schmidt_Lehrer-Student.jpg/500px-Rostock_Schmidt_Lehrer-Student.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1322" data-file-height="1763" /></a><figcaption>The teacher-student-monument in <a href="/wiki/Rostock" title="Rostock">Rostock</a>, Germany, honors teachers.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Teachers facilitate student learning, often in a school or academy or perhaps in another environment such as <a href="/wiki/Outdoor_education" title="Outdoor education">outdoors</a>.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-13055-0008,_Hohendorf,_JP_mit_Dorflehrer.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-13055-0008%2C_Hohendorf%2C_JP_mit_Dorflehrer.jpg/250px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-13055-0008%2C_Hohendorf%2C_JP_mit_Dorflehrer.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="190" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-13055-0008%2C_Hohendorf%2C_JP_mit_Dorflehrer.jpg/500px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-13055-0008%2C_Hohendorf%2C_JP_mit_Dorflehrer.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="760" data-file-height="578" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/East_Germany" title="East Germany">GDR</a> "village teacher", a teacher teaching students of all age groups in one class in 1951</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Jewish_Children_with_their_Teacher_in_Samarkand_cropped.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Jewish_Children_with_their_Teacher_in_Samarkand_cropped.jpg/250px-Jewish_Children_with_their_Teacher_in_Samarkand_cropped.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="236" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Jewish_Children_with_their_Teacher_in_Samarkand_cropped.jpg/500px-Jewish_Children_with_their_Teacher_in_Samarkand_cropped.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="1914" data-file-height="1809" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Jew" class="mw-redirect" title="Jew">Jewish</a> children with their teacher in <a href="/wiki/Samarkand" title="Samarkand">Samarkand</a>, the beginning of the 20th century</figcaption></figure>
<p>The objective is typically accomplished through either an <a href="/wiki/Informal_learning" title="Informal learning">informal</a> or formal approach to learning, including a course of study and <a href="/wiki/Lesson_plan" title="Lesson plan">lesson plan</a> that teaches <a href="/wiki/Skill" title="Skill">skills</a>, <a href="/wiki/Knowledge" title="Knowledge">knowledge</a> or <a href="/wiki/Thinking" class="mw-redirect" title="Thinking">thinking</a> skills. Different ways to teach are often referred to as <a href="/wiki/Pedagogy" title="Pedagogy">pedagogy</a>. When deciding what teaching method to use teachers consider students' background knowledge, environment, and their learning goals as well as standardized <a href="/wiki/Curricula" class="mw-redirect" title="Curricula">curricula</a> as determined by the relevant authority. Many times, teachers assist in learning outside of the classroom by accompanying students on field trips. The increasing use of <a href="/wiki/Technology" title="Technology">technology</a>, specifically the rise of the <a href="/wiki/Internet" title="Internet">internet</a> over the past decade, has begun to shape the way teachers approach their roles in the <a href="/wiki/Classroom" title="Classroom">classroom</a>.
</p><p>The objective is typically a course of study, <a href="/wiki/Lesson_plan" title="Lesson plan">lesson plan</a>, or a practical skill. A teacher may follow standardized <a href="/wiki/Curriculum" title="Curriculum">curricula</a> as determined by the relevant authority. The teacher may interact with students of different ages, from infants to adults, students with different abilities and students with learning <a href="/wiki/Disabilities" class="mw-redirect" title="Disabilities">disabilities</a>.
</p><p>Teaching using pedagogy also involve assessing the educational levels of the students on particular skills. Understanding the pedagogy of the students in a classroom involves using <a href="/wiki/Differentiated_instruction" title="Differentiated instruction">differentiated instruction</a> as well as supervision to meet the needs of all students in the classroom. Pedagogy can be thought of in two manners. Teaching itself can be taught in many different ways, hence, using a pedagogy of <a href="/wiki/Teaching_method" title="Teaching method">teaching methods</a>. For example, an experienced teacher and parent described the place of a teacher in learning as follows: "The real bulk of learning takes place in self-study and problem solving with a lot of feedback around that loop. The function of the teacher is to pressure the lazy, inspire the bored, deflate the cocky, encourage the timid, detect and correct individual flaws, and broaden the viewpoint of all. This function looks like that of a coach using the whole gamut of psychology to get each new class of rookies off the bench and into the game."<sup id="cite&#95;ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>36<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Perhaps the most significant difference between <a href="/wiki/Primary_school" title="Primary school">primary school</a> and <a href="/wiki/Secondary_school" title="Secondary school">secondary school</a> teaching is the relationship between teachers and children. In primary schools each class has a teacher who stays with them for most of the week and will teach them the whole curriculum. In secondary schools they will be taught by different subject specialists each session during the week and may have ten or more different teachers. The relationship between children and their teachers tends to be closer in the primary school where they act as form tutor, specialist teacher and surrogate parent during the course of the day.
</p><p>This is true throughout most of the United States as well. However, alternative approaches for primary education do exist. One of these, sometimes referred to as a "platoon" system, involves placing a group of students together in one class that moves from one specialist to another for every subject. The advantage here is that students learn from teachers who specialize in one subject and who tend to be more knowledgeable in that one area than a teacher who teaches many subjects. Students still derive a strong sense of security by staying with the same group of peers for all classes.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Co-teaching" title="Co-teaching">Co-teaching</a> has also become a new trend amongst educational institutions. Co-teaching is defined as two or more teachers working harmoniously to fulfill the needs of every student in the classroom. Co-teaching focuses the student on learning by providing a <a href="/wiki/Social_networking" class="mw-redirect" title="Social networking">social networking</a> support that allows them to reach their full cognitive potential. Co-teachers work in sync with one another to create a climate of learning.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Classroom_management">Classroom management</h3></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Teachers_and_school_discipline">Teachers and school discipline</h4></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/School_discipline" title="School discipline">School discipline</a>, <a href="/wiki/School_punishment" class="mw-redirect" title="School punishment">School punishment</a>, and <a href="/wiki/School_corporal_punishment" class="mw-redirect" title="School corporal punishment">School corporal punishment</a></div>
<p><br />
</p><p>
Throughout the <a href="/wiki/History_of_education" title="History of education">history of education</a> the most common form of <a href="/wiki/School_discipline" title="School discipline">school discipline</a> was <a href="/wiki/School_corporal_punishment" class="mw-redirect" title="School corporal punishment">corporal punishment</a>. While a child was in school, a teacher was expected to act as a <a href="/wiki/In_loco_parentis" title="In loco parentis">substitute parent</a>, with all the normal forms of parental discipline open to them. </p><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Koerperstrafe-_MA_Birkenrute.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/1/11/Koerperstrafe-_MA_Birkenrute.png" decoding="async" width="120" height="170" class="mw-file-element" data-file-width="120" data-file-height="170" /></a><figcaption>Medieval schoolboy birched on the bare buttocks</figcaption></figure>
<p>In past times, corporal punishment (<a href="/wiki/Spanking" title="Spanking">spanking</a> or <a href="/wiki/Paddle_(spanking)" class="mw-redirect" title="Paddle (spanking)">paddling</a> or <a href="/wiki/Caning" title="Caning">caning</a> or <a href="/wiki/Strapping_(punishment)" title="Strapping (punishment)">strapping</a> or <a href="/wiki/Birching" title="Birching">birching</a> the student in order to cause physical pain) was one of the most common forms of school discipline throughout much of the world. Most Western countries, and some others, have now banned it, but it remains lawful in the United States following <a href="/wiki/Ingraham_v._Wright" title="Ingraham v. Wright">a US Supreme Court decision</a> in 1977 which held that paddling did not violate the <a href="/wiki/US_Constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="US Constitution">US Constitution</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>37<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>30 US states have banned corporal punishment, the others (mostly in <a href="/wiki/Southern_United_States" title="Southern United States">the South</a>) have not. It is still used to a significant (though declining) degree in some public schools in <a href="/wiki/Alabama" title="Alabama">Alabama</a>, <a href="/wiki/Arkansas" title="Arkansas">Arkansas</a>, <a href="/wiki/Georgia_(U.S._state)" title="Georgia (U.S. state)">Georgia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Louisiana" title="Louisiana">Louisiana</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mississippi" title="Mississippi">Mississippi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Oklahoma" title="Oklahoma">Oklahoma</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tennessee" title="Tennessee">Tennessee</a> and <a href="/wiki/Texas" title="Texas">Texas</a>. Private schools in these and most other states may also use it. Corporal punishment in American schools is administered to the seat of the student's trousers or skirt with a specially made wooden paddle. This often used to take place in the classroom or hallway, but nowadays the punishment is usually given privately in the principal's office.
</p><p>Official corporal punishment, often by caning, remains commonplace in schools in some Asian, African and Caribbean countries.
</p><p>Currently detention is one of the most common punishments in schools in the United States, the UK, Ireland, Singapore and other countries. It requires the pupil to remain in school at a given time in the school day (such as lunch, recess or after school); or even to attend school on a non-school day, e.g. "Saturday detention" held at some schools. During detention, students normally have to sit in a classroom and do work, write lines or a punishment essay, or sit quietly.
</p><p>A modern example of school discipline in North America and Western Europe relies upon the idea of an assertive teacher who is prepared to impose their will upon a class. Positive reinforcement is balanced with immediate and fair punishment for misbehavior and firm, clear boundaries define what is appropriate and inappropriate behavior.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>38<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Teachers are expected to respect their students; sarcasm and attempts to humiliate pupils are seen as falling outside of what constitutes reasonable discipline.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>39<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Whilst this is the consensus viewpoint amongst the majority of academics, some teachers and parents advocate a more assertive and confrontational style of discipline<sup id="cite&#95;ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>40<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> (refer to <i>Canter Model of Discipline</i>).<sup id="cite&#95;ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>41<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Such individuals claim that many problems with modern schooling stem from the weakness in school discipline and if teachers exercised firm control over the classroom they would be able to teach more efficiently. This viewpoint is supported by the educational attainment of countries—in East Asia for instance—that combine strict discipline with high standards of education.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>42<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>43<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>44<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>It's not clear, however that this stereotypical view reflects the reality of East Asian classrooms or that the educational goals in these countries are commensurable with those in Western countries. In <a href="/wiki/Japan" title="Japan">Japan</a>, for example, although average attainment on standardized tests may exceed those in Western countries, classroom discipline and behavior is highly problematic. Although, officially, schools have extremely rigid codes of behavior, in practice many teachers find the students unmanageable and do not enforce discipline at all.
</p><p>Where school class sizes are typically 40 to 50 students, maintaining order in the classroom can divert the teacher from instruction, leaving little opportunity for concentration and focus on what is being taught. In response, teachers may concentrate their attention on motivated students, ignoring <a href="/wiki/Attention-seeking" class="mw-redirect" title="Attention-seeking">attention-seeking</a> and disruptive students. The result of this is that motivated students, facing demanding university entrance examinations, receive disproportionate resources. Given the emphasis on attainment of university places, administrators and governors may regard this policy as appropriate.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Obligation_to_honor_students_rights">Obligation to honor students rights</h4></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/School_discipline#Discipline_in_Sudbury-model_democratic_schools" title="School discipline">Discipline in Sudbury-model democratic schools</a></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Sudbury_model" class="mw-redirect" title="Sudbury model">Sudbury-model</a> democratic schools claim that popularly based authority can maintain order more effectively than dictatorial authority for governments and schools alike. They also claim that in these schools the preservation of public order is easier and more efficient than anywhere else. Primarily because rules and regulations are made by the community as a whole, thence the school atmosphere is one of persuasion and negotiation, rather than confrontation since there is no one to confront. Sudbury model democratic schools' proponents argue that a school that has good, clear laws, fairly and democratically passed by the entire school community, and a good judicial system for enforcing these laws, is a school in which community discipline prevails, and in which an increasingly sophisticated concept of law and order develops, against other schools today, where rules are arbitrary, authority is absolute, punishment is capricious, and <a href="/wiki/Due_process_of_law" class="mw-redirect" title="Due process of law">due process of law</a> is unknown.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>45<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>46<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Occupational_hazards">Occupational hazards</h2></div>
<p>Teachers face several <a href="/wiki/Occupational_hazard" title="Occupational hazard">occupational hazards</a> in their line of work, including <a href="/wiki/Occupational_stress" title="Occupational stress">occupational stress</a>, which can negatively impact teachers' mental and physical health, productivity, and students' performance. Stress can be caused by organizational change, relationships with students, fellow teachers, and administrative personnel, working environment, expectations to substitute, long hours with a heavy workload, and inspections. Teachers are also at high risk for <a href="/wiki/Occupational_burnout" title="Occupational burnout">occupational burnout</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:0&#95;47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-47"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>47<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>A 2000 study found that 42% of UK teachers experienced occupational stress, twice the figure for the average profession. A 2012 study found that teachers experienced double the rate of anxiety, depression, and stress than average workers.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:0&#95;47-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-47"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>47<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>There are several ways to mitigate the occupational hazards of teaching. Organizational interventions, like changing teachers' schedules, providing support networks and mentoring, changing the work environment, and offering promotions and bonuses, may be effective in helping to reduce occupational stress among teachers. Individual-level interventions, including stress-management training and counseling, are also used to relieve occupational stress among teachers.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:0&#95;47-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-47"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>47<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Apart from this, teachers are often not given sufficient opportunities for professional growth or promotions. This leads to some stagnancy, as there is not sufficient interests to enter the profession. An organisation in India called <a href="/w/index.php?title=Centre_for_Teacher_Accreditation&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Centre for Teacher Accreditation (page does not exist)">Centre for Teacher Accreditation (CENTA)</a> is working to reduce this hazard, by trying to open opportunities for teachers in India.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Teaching_around_the_world">Teaching around the world<span class="anchor" id="By&#95;country"></span></h2></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ASC_Leiden_-_Coutinho_Collection_-_6_24_-_School_for_adults_in_Guinea-Bissau_-_1974.tiff" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/ASC_Leiden_-_Coutinho_Collection_-_6_24_-_School_for_adults_in_Guinea-Bissau_-_1974.tiff/lossy-page1-250px-ASC_Leiden_-_Coutinho_Collection_-_6_24_-_School_for_adults_in_Guinea-Bissau_-_1974.tiff.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="168" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/ASC_Leiden_-_Coutinho_Collection_-_6_24_-_School_for_adults_in_Guinea-Bissau_-_1974.tiff/lossy-page1-500px-ASC_Leiden_-_Coutinho_Collection_-_6_24_-_School_for_adults_in_Guinea-Bissau_-_1974.tiff.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="4755" data-file-height="3204" /></a><figcaption>Teacher and pupils in liberated <a href="/wiki/Guinea-Bissau" title="Guinea-Bissau">Guinea-Bissau</a>, 1974</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Agnar_O.P._Strandberg_1964.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Agnar_O.P._Strandberg_1964.jpg/250px-Agnar_O.P._Strandberg_1964.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="235" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Agnar_O.P._Strandberg_1964.jpg/330px-Agnar_O.P._Strandberg_1964.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Agnar_O.P._Strandberg_1964.jpg/500px-Agnar_O.P._Strandberg_1964.jpg 2x" data-file-width="899" data-file-height="1176" /></a><figcaption>Math and physics teacher at a junior college in <a href="/wiki/Sweden" title="Sweden">Sweden</a>, in the 1960s</figcaption></figure>
<p>There are many similarities and differences among teachers around the world. In almost all countries teachers are educated in a university or college. Governments may require <a href="/wiki/Certified_teacher" title="Certified teacher">certification</a> by a recognized body before they can teach in a school. In many countries, elementary school education certificate is earned after completion of high school. The high school student follows an education specialty track, obtain the prerequisite "student-teaching" time, and receive a special diploma to begin teaching after graduation. In addition to certification, many educational institutions especially within the US, require that prospective teachers pass a background check and psychiatric evaluation to be able to teach in classroom. This is not always the case with adult further learning institutions but is fast becoming the norm in many countries as security<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Osborne,&#95;E.&#95;1999&#95;24-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Osborne,_E._1999-24"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>24<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> concerns grow.
</p><p>In most countries women are overrepresented among teachers at <a href="/wiki/Primary_school" title="Primary school">primary schools</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-y608&#95;48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-y608-48"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>48<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Secondary_school" title="Secondary school">secondary schools</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-b454&#95;49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-b454-49"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>49<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/International_school" title="International school">International schools</a> generally follow an English-speaking, Western curriculum and are aimed at expatriate communities.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-intschools&#95;50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-intschools-50"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>50<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Australia">Australia</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_Australia" title="Education in Australia">Education in Australia</a></div>
<p>Education in Australia is primarily the responsibility of the individual <a href="/wiki/States_and_territories_of_Australia" title="States and territories of Australia">states and territories</a>. Generally, education in Australia follows the three-tier model which includes <a href="/wiki/Primary_education" title="Primary education">primary education</a> (primary schools), followed by <a href="/wiki/Secondary_education" title="Secondary education">secondary education</a> (secondary schools/high schools) and <a href="/wiki/Tertiary_education" title="Tertiary education">tertiary education</a> (universities or <a href="/wiki/Technical_and_further_education" title="Technical and further education">TAFE</a> colleges).
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Canada">Canada</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_Canada" title="Education in Canada">Education in Canada</a></div>
<p>Teaching in <a href="/wiki/Canada" title="Canada">Canada</a> requires a post-secondary degree <a href="/wiki/Bachelor%27s_Degree" class="mw-redirect" title="Bachelor&#39;s Degree">Bachelor's Degree</a>. In most provinces a second <a href="/wiki/Bachelor%27s_Degree" class="mw-redirect" title="Bachelor&#39;s Degree">Bachelor's Degree</a> such as a <a href="/wiki/Bachelor_of_Education" title="Bachelor of Education">Bachelor of Education</a> is required to become a qualified teacher. Salary ranges from $40,000/year to $90,000/yr. Teachers have the option to teach for a public school which is funded by the provincial government or teaching in a private school which is funded by the private sector, businesses and sponsors.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="France">France</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_France" title="Education in France">Education in France</a></div>
<p>In <a href="/wiki/France" title="France">France</a>, teachers, or <i>professors</i>, are mainly civil servants, recruited by <a href="/wiki/Competitive_examination" class="mw-redirect" title="Competitive examination">competitive examination</a>.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Germany">Germany</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_Germany" title="Education in Germany">Education in Germany</a></div>
<p>In <a href="/wiki/Germany" title="Germany">Germany</a>, teachers are mainly civil servants recruited in special university classes, called <i>Lehramtstudien</i> (<i>Teaching Education Studies</i>). There are many differences between the teachers for elementary schools (<i>Grundschule</i>), lower secondary schools (<i>Hauptschule</i>), middle level secondary schools (<i>Realschule</i>) and higher level secondary schools (<i>Gymnasium</i>).
Salaries for teachers depend on the civil servants' salary index scale (<i>Bundesbesoldungsordnung</i>).
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="India">India</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Gurukula" title="Gurukula">Gurukula</a> and <a href="/wiki/Education_in_India" title="Education in India">Education in India</a></div>
<p>In ancient India, the most common form of education was <a href="/wiki/Gurukula" title="Gurukula">gurukula</a> based on the <a href="/wiki/Guru%E2%80%93shishya_tradition" title="Guru–shishya tradition">guru-shishya tradition</a> (teacher-disciple tradition) which involved the disciple and guru living in the same (or a nearby) residence. These <i>gurukulam</i> was supported by public donations and the guru would not accept any fees from the <i>shishya</i>. This organized system stayed the most prominent form of education in the <a href="/wiki/Indian_subcontinent" title="Indian subcontinent">Indian subcontinent</a> until the British invasion. Through strong efforts in 1886 and 1948, the gurukula system was revived in India.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>51<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>52<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The role and success of a teacher in the modern Indian education system is clearly defined. <a href="/w/index.php?title=Centre_for_Teacher_Accreditation_(CENTA)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Centre for Teacher Accreditation (CENTA) (page does not exist)">CENTA</a> Standards define the competencies that a good teacher should possess. Schools look for competent teachers across grades. Teachers are appointed directly by schools in private sector, and through eligibility tests in government schools.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Ireland">Ireland</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_the_Republic_of_Ireland" title="Education in the Republic of Ireland">Education in the Republic of Ireland</a></div>
<p>Salaries for primary teachers in <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Ireland" title="Republic of Ireland">Ireland</a> depend mainly on seniority (i.e. holding the position of <a href="/wiki/Principal_(school)" class="mw-redirect" title="Principal (school)">principal</a>, deputy principal or <a href="/wiki/Assistant_principal" class="mw-redirect" title="Assistant principal">assistant principal</a>), experience and qualifications. Extra pay is also given for teaching through the <a href="/wiki/Irish_language" title="Irish language">Irish language</a>, in a <a href="/wiki/Gaeltacht" title="Gaeltacht">Gaeltacht</a> area or on an <a href="/wiki/Islands_of_Ireland" class="mw-redirect" title="Islands of Ireland">island</a>. The basic pay for a starting teacher is €27,814 <a href="/wiki/Per_annum" title="Per annum">p.a.</a>, rising incrementally to €53,423 for a teacher with 25 years service. A principal of a large school with many years experience and several qualifications (<a href="/wiki/Master_of_Arts" title="Master of Arts">M.A.</a>, <a href="/wiki/H.Dip." class="mw-redirect" title="H.Dip.">H.Dip.</a>, etc.) could earn over €90,000.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>53<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Teachers are required to be registered with the Teaching Council; under Section 30 of the Teaching Council Act 2001, a person employed in any capacity in a recognised teaching post - who is not registered with the Teaching Council - may not be paid from <a href="/wiki/Oireachtas" title="Oireachtas">Oireachtas</a> funds.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>54<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>55<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>From 2006 <a href="/wiki/Garda_S%C3%ADoch%C3%A1na" title="Garda Síochána">Garda</a> vetting has been introduced for new entrants to the teaching profession. These procedures apply to teaching and also to non-teaching posts and those who refuse vetting "cannot be appointed or engaged by the school in any capacity including in a voluntary role". Existing staff will be vetted on a phased basis.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>56<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>57<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Philippines">Philippines</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_the_Philippines" title="Education in the Philippines">Education in the Philippines</a></div>
<p>To become a teacher in the Philippines, one must have a bachelor's degree in education. Other degrees are also allowed as long they are able to get 18 units of professional education subjects (10 units for arts and sciences degrees). A board exam must be taken to become a professional teacher in the Philippines. Upon passing the board exam, the <a href="/wiki/Professional_Regulatory_Commission" class="mw-redirect" title="Professional Regulatory Commission">Professional Regulatory Commission</a> will issue a teaching licence.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>58<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>59<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="United_Kingdom">United Kingdom</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Education in the United Kingdom">Education in the United Kingdom</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Schoolmarm,_a_figurine_by_Royal_Doulton.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b1/Schoolmarm%2C_a_figurine_by_Royal_Doulton.jpg/250px-Schoolmarm%2C_a_figurine_by_Royal_Doulton.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="333" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b1/Schoolmarm%2C_a_figurine_by_Royal_Doulton.jpg/500px-Schoolmarm%2C_a_figurine_by_Royal_Doulton.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="3000" data-file-height="4000" /></a><figcaption>Schoolmarm, a figurine by <a href="/wiki/Royal_Doulton" title="Royal Doulton">Royal Doulton</a> </figcaption></figure>
<p>Education in the United Kingdom is a <a href="/wiki/Devolution" title="Devolution">devolved matter</a> with each of the <a href="/wiki/Countries_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Countries of the United Kingdom">countries of the United Kingdom</a> having separate systems.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="England">England</h4></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_England" title="Education in England">Education in England</a></div>
<p>Salaries for <a href="/wiki/Nursery_school" class="mw-redirect" title="Nursery school">nursery</a>, primary and secondary school teachers ranged from <a href="/wiki/GBP" class="mw-redirect" title="GBP">£</a>20,133 to £41,004 in September 2007, although some salaries can go much higher depending on experience and extra responsibilities.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>60<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Preschool teachers may earn an average salary of £19,543 annually.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>61<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Teachers in state schools must have at least a <a href="/wiki/Bachelor%27s_degree" title="Bachelor&#39;s degree">bachelor's degree</a>, complete an approved teacher education program, and be licensed.
</p><p>Many counties offer alternative licensing programs to attract people into teaching, especially for hard-to-fill positions. Excellent job opportunities are expected as <a href="/wiki/Retirement" title="Retirement">retirements</a>, especially among secondary school teachers, outweigh slowing enrollment growth; opportunities will vary by geographic area and subject taught.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2008)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Scotland">Scotland</h4></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_Scotland" title="Education in Scotland">Education in Scotland</a></div>
<p>In <a href="/wiki/Scotland" title="Scotland">Scotland</a>, anyone wishing to teach must be registered with the <a href="/wiki/General_Teaching_Council_for_Scotland" title="General Teaching Council for Scotland">General Teaching Council for Scotland</a> (GTCS). Teaching in Scotland is an all graduate profession and the normal route for graduates wishing to teach is to complete a programme of Initial Teacher Education (ITE) at one of the seven Scottish Universities who offer these courses. Once successfully completed, "Provisional Registration" is given by the GTCS which is raised to "Full Registration" status after a year if there is sufficient evidence to show that the "Standard for Full Registration" has been met.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>62<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>For the salary year beginning April 2008, unpromoted teachers in <a href="/wiki/Scotland" title="Scotland">Scotland</a> earned from £20,427 for a Probationer, up to £32,583 after 6 years teaching, but could then go on to earn up to £39,942 as they complete the modules to earn Chartered Teacher Status (requiring at least 6 years at up to two modules per year.) Promotion to <a href="/wiki/Principal_Teacher" class="mw-redirect" title="Principal Teacher">Principal Teacher</a> positions attracts a salary of between £34,566 and £44,616; Deputy Head, and <a href="/wiki/Head_teacher" title="Head teacher">Head teachers</a> earn from £40,290 to £78,642.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>63<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
Teachers in Scotland can be registered members of trade unions with the main ones being the <a href="/wiki/Educational_Institute_of_Scotland" title="Educational Institute of Scotland">Educational Institute of Scotland</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Scottish_Secondary_Teachers%27_Association" title="Scottish Secondary Teachers&#39; Association">Scottish Secondary Teachers' Association</a>.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Wales">Wales</h4></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_Wales" title="Education in Wales">Education in Wales</a></div>
<p>Education in Wales differs in certain respects from education elsewhere in the United Kingdom. For example, a significant number of students all over Wales are educated either wholly or largely through the medium of Welsh: in 2008/09, 22 per cent of classes in maintained primary schools used Welsh as the sole or main medium of instruction. Welsh medium education is available to all age groups through nurseries, schools, colleges and universities and in adult education; lessons in the language itself are compulsory for all pupils until the age of 16.
</p><p>Teachers in <a href="/wiki/Wales" title="Wales">Wales</a> can be registered members of <a href="/wiki/Trade_unions" class="mw-redirect" title="Trade unions">trade unions</a> such as <a href="/wiki/Association_of_Teachers_and_Lecturers" title="Association of Teachers and Lecturers">ATL</a>, <a href="/wiki/National_Union_of_Teachers" title="National Union of Teachers">NUT</a> or <a href="/wiki/NASUWT" title="NASUWT">NASUWT</a> and a report in 2010 suggested that the average age of teachers in Wales was falling with teachers being younger than in previous years. It was suggested that a proportion of older teachers had faced discrimination and did not have their experience valued.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>64<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> A growing cause of concern at that time was that attacks on teachers in Welsh schools reached an all-time high between 2005 and 2010.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>65<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="United_States">United States</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_the_United_States" title="Education in the United States">Education in the United States</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Paraprofessional_educator" title="Paraprofessional educator">Paraprofessional educator</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:NOLA_Experience_-_Tulane_University_Students_and_Teacher_with_Cannon,_New_Orleans,_2009.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/NOLA_Experience_-_Tulane_University_Students_and_Teacher_with_Cannon%2C_New_Orleans%2C_2009.jpg/250px-NOLA_Experience_-_Tulane_University_Students_and_Teacher_with_Cannon%2C_New_Orleans%2C_2009.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="174" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/NOLA_Experience_-_Tulane_University_Students_and_Teacher_with_Cannon%2C_New_Orleans%2C_2009.jpg/500px-NOLA_Experience_-_Tulane_University_Students_and_Teacher_with_Cannon%2C_New_Orleans%2C_2009.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="3988" data-file-height="2776" /></a><figcaption>Students of a U.S. university with their professor on the far right, 2009</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the United States, each <a href="/wiki/U.S._state" title="U.S. state">state</a> determines the requirements for getting a license to teach in public schools. Teaching certification generally lasts three years, but teachers can receive certificates that last as long as ten years.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>66<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Public school teachers are required to have a bachelor's degree and the majority must be certified by the state in which they teach. Many <a href="/wiki/Charter_schools" class="mw-redirect" title="Charter schools">charter schools</a> do not require that their teachers be certified, provided they meet the standards to be highly qualified as set by <a href="/wiki/No_Child_Left_Behind" class="mw-redirect" title="No Child Left Behind">No Child Left Behind</a>. Additionally, the requirements for substitute/temporary teachers are generally not as rigorous as those for full-time professionals. The <a href="/wiki/Bureau_of_Labor_Statistics" title="Bureau of Labor Statistics">Bureau of Labor Statistics</a> estimates that there are 1.4 million <a href="/wiki/Elementary_school" class="mw-redirect" title="Elementary school">elementary school</a> teachers,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>67<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> 674,000 <a href="/wiki/Middle_school" title="Middle school">middle school</a> teachers,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>68<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and 1 million <a href="/wiki/Secondary_school" title="Secondary school">secondary school</a> teachers employed in the U.S.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>69<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>In the past, teachers have been paid relatively low salaries. However, average teacher salaries have improved rapidly in recent years. US teachers are generally paid on graduated scales, with income depending on experience. Teachers with more experience and higher education earn more than those with a standard bachelor's degree and certificate. Salaries vary greatly depending on state, relative cost of living, and grade taught. Salaries also vary within states where wealthy suburban school districts generally have higher salary schedules than other districts. The median salary for all primary and secondary teachers was <a href="/wiki/USD" class="mw-redirect" title="USD">$</a>46,000 in 2004, with the average entry salary for a teacher with a bachelor's degree being an estimated $32,000. Median salaries for preschool teachers, however, were less than half the national median for secondary teachers, clock in at an estimated $21,000 in 2004.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>70<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>71<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-US&#95;Salaries,&#95;2004&#95;72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-US_Salaries,_2004-72"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>72<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> For high school teachers, median salaries in 2007 ranged from $35,000 in South Dakota to $71,000 in New York, with a national median of $52,000.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-US&#95;Salaries,&#95;2007&#95;73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-US_Salaries,_2007-73"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>73<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Some contracts may include long-term <a href="/wiki/Disability_insurance" title="Disability insurance">disability insurance</a>, <a href="/wiki/Life_insurance" title="Life insurance">life insurance</a>, emergency/<a href="/wiki/Leave_of_absence" title="Leave of absence">personal leave</a> and investment options.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>74<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:A_college_professor_teaching_in_a_university_classroom_full_of_students_in_Tennessee,_United_States_02.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/A_college_professor_teaching_in_a_university_classroom_full_of_students_in_Tennessee%2C_United_States_02.jpg/250px-A_college_professor_teaching_in_a_university_classroom_full_of_students_in_Tennessee%2C_United_States_02.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="167" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/A_college_professor_teaching_in_a_university_classroom_full_of_students_in_Tennessee%2C_United_States_02.jpg/500px-A_college_professor_teaching_in_a_university_classroom_full_of_students_in_Tennessee%2C_United_States_02.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="3456" data-file-height="2304" /></a><figcaption>A teacher holds a class in <a href="/wiki/Cleveland,_Tennessee" title="Cleveland, Tennessee">Cleveland, Tennessee</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/American_Federation_of_Teachers" title="American Federation of Teachers">American Federation of Teachers</a>' teacher salary survey for the 2006–07 school year found that the average teacher salary was $51,009.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>75<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In a salary survey report for K-12 teachers, elementary school teachers had the lowest median salary earning $39,259. High school teachers had the highest median salary earning $41,855.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>76<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Many teachers take advantage of the opportunity to increase their income by supervising after-school programs and other extracurricular activities. In addition to monetary compensation, public school teachers may also enjoy greater benefits (like health insurance) compared to other occupations. Merit pay systems are on the rise for teachers, paying teachers extra money based on excellent classroom evaluations, high test scores and for high success at their overall school. Also, with the advent of the internet, many teachers are now selling their lesson plans to other teachers through the web in order to earn supplemental income, most notably on TeachersPayTeachers.com.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>77<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The United Nations <a href="/wiki/Sustainable_Development_Goal_4" title="Sustainable Development Goal 4">Sustainable Development Goal 4</a> also aims to substantially increase the supply of qualified teachers through international cooperation by 2030 in an effort to improve the quality of teaching around the world.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>78<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
